Lights are not decoration; they are the readout.

**Visual:** activity LEDs flicker at a rate proportional to real load. Drive lights show reads/writes.
Link lights show negotiated speed by colour. A failed unit shows a solid amber fault LED. **The room's
overall light pattern tells you how the business is doing before you read a single number.**

### Cable colour coding
Colour is a language, and nobody agreed on it.

**Visual:** the player picks a convention (or inherits a mess in acquisition levels). Consistent
colour-coding gives a small MTTR bonus. **The joke and the mechanic are the same thing** (§9.3).

### The power overlay
Electricity made visible.

**Visual:** in power view, feeds glow gold from the utility through the transfer switch, UPS, PDUs and
into each device, with thickness ∝ amps. Circuits near their derate limit pulse amber. **A failure here
is a light going out and a chain going dark upstream of everything it fed.**

### Cooling and CRAC units
Air as a visible medium.

**Visual:** faint cold-air mist from perforated floor tiles, heat shimmer above hot aisles, containment
curtains, fan blur. When cooling fails, **the shimmer grows and spreads and the room's colour
temperature climbs** — a five-minute-long visual warning before anything throttles.

### The generator and the lighting shift
Power loss is a whole-scene event.

**Visual:** utility fails → the room drops to **UPS battery lighting** (dimmer, cooler, with a very
distinct emergency tint) → the generator starts (a shudder, then warm light returns but slightly
different in colour) → transfer. **The entire scene's lighting narrates the power chain**, and the
battery-lit interval is the most tense-looking thing in the game.

### The internet cloud / spawn edge
Where traffic comes from.

**Visual:** a stylized boundary at the edge of the board — a cloud, a horizon, a bank of upstream
routers — from which visitors and threats emerge. At Z4 it becomes actual geography.

### Cross-connect ladder racking
The colo signature image.

**Visual:** overhead ladder racking dense with orange fibre jumpers running to the meet-me room. **Each
strand is money** (§6.6), and a well-populated meet-me room should look obviously valuable.

### Wear, grime, and dust
Time made visible.

**Visual:** dust accumulates on unmaintained gear, filters discolour, labels yellow and peel, cable ties
get replaced with tape. **A clean room and a filthy room should be instantly distinguishable**, and the
maintenance-debt overlay (§7.6) is just this turned up.

### The two registers: Room vs Board
The game renders in two visual registers and you switch between them.

**How it works:** **The Room** is the physical diorama — warm, lit, material, where you feel the
business. **The Board** is the topology/schematic — flat, cool, precise, where you reason about it.
**The same state, two languages**, and the transition between them should be an animated fold rather
than a cut.

---

## 8.5 What threats look like

*Per-threat visuals live with each threat in §2. The shared rules:*

### The threat visual contract
Every threat is readable in four ways.

**How it works:** **Silhouette** (family), **colour** (magenta family, with a hue shift per class),
**motion signature** (how it moves is how it behaves), and **telegraph** (how it announces itself before
it lands). **You must be able to identify a threat class from the silhouette at Z3 with the colour
removed.**

### Motion as behaviour
The animation *is* the mechanic.

**How it works:** A volumetric flood is a **wall** that advances. A slow-drip attack is a **thin thread**
that barely moves. A scanner **sweeps** methodically. A bot swarm **clusters and disperses.** A mimic
**moves exactly like a visitor until it doesn't.** An insider threat **originates inside the board.**
Ransomware **crystallizes** outward from a point. **Never explain a threat's behaviour in text if you can
show it in motion.**

### The Threat Gantry
The bestiary as portraits.

**Visual:** each threat has a card-art portrait in a consistent frame — a specimen under glass, with its
name, class, first-encountered date and lifetime cost. **The single best art showcase in the game**
(§5.4), and it earns itself through play.

### Telegraphs
Nothing lands without warning.

**Visual:** a horizon glow before a flood; a rising audio tone; radar contacts on the edge; the forecast
widget (§7.9). **The size of the telegraph is proportional to the threat**, so a boss announces itself
for a long time.

### The unidentified state
Threats arrive violet.

**Visual:** an unclassified contact is a violet blob with an indistinct silhouette; as inspection layers
work on it, the silhouette resolves and the colour snaps to its true class with a small **identification
flash.** **Classification is the most-repeated visual event in the game and it should feel great.**

### Attack landing
Impact needs weight.

**Visual:** a magenta strike against a node produces a shield ripple (blocked), a flinch and a red
crack (damaged), or a hard slam and a brief desaturation (breach). **Screen-shake is reserved for
genuine severity**, per the Readability Budget.

### Persistence and infestation
Some threats stay.

**Visual:** a compromised node grows a subtle corruption — a dark vein pattern, a flickering wrongness —
that spreads along trust links if untreated. **Visible slow corruption is far scarier than a damage
number.**

---

## 8.6 What visitors look like

### The patience ring
The single most important visitor visual.

**Visual:** every visitor carries a thin ring that depletes as it waits. Full ring = happy; depleting =
impatient; empty = gone. **Readable at any zoom as a silhouette**, and the whole latency economy (§3.1)
is legible from it.

### Segment-coded appearance
Who they are is visible.

**Visual:** costume/colour variations by customer segment — the hobbyist, the agency, the enterprise
buyer, the whale (a diamond, larger, brighter, with a slower patience ring but a much bigger bounty).
**Value as size and glow** is the simplest possible legibility for "this one matters."

### The conversion moment
The payoff beat.

**Visual:** a visitor reaching the goal node performs a small satisfying flourish and emits a **gold coin
mote** that arcs to the money counter (**FX_CoinArc**). **This is the reward loop's entire visual
language and it happens thousands of times, so it must be tiny, crisp, and never annoying.**

### The bounce
The loss beat.

**Visual:** patience ring empties → the visitor **dissolves into a puff and drifts backward** off the
board (**FX_BouncePuff**), leaving a faint grey mark on the path where it gave up. **A trail of grey
marks along a slow path is the best diagnostic in the game.**

### The false-positive flash
The most important negative feedback in the document.

**Visual:** when a defense blocks a legitimate visitor, the blocked entity flashes **amber** rather than
magenta, and a small amber tick appears on the defense that did it. **You should feel a tiny wince every
time**, because that was a paying customer.

### Word-of-mouth tokens
Satisfaction propagates.

**Visual:** a delighted customer emits a small green mote that travels to the spawn edge and returns
later with a friend. **Retention rendered as a visible loop** rather than a percentage.

### Crowd density as a particle field
At scale, individuals become flow.

**How it works:** Below a threshold, discrete entities with rings. Above it, a **continuous particle
stream whose density, colour and velocity carry the same information.** The transition is smooth and
automatic. **The only way to render a million requests a second without lying.**

### Session and duration classes
Not all visitors are instants.

**Visual:** a page load is a dot. A game player is a **teardrop that persists** at the node for the
length of their session. A backup job is a **square that grows** as it transfers. A SIP call is a
**line held between two points.** An inference request is a **dot that sits and glows while it
computes.** **The shape of the visitor tells you the shape of the business** (§3.3).

---

## 8.7 Expressing actions, money, and state

### Traffic as light
The core visual metaphor.

**Visual:** requests are luminous motes travelling along cables. Throughput is **density and brightness**;
latency is **speed**; queueing is **bunching**; failure is **motes winking out**. **One metaphor carries
the entire performance story.**

### The queue as physical stacking
Waiting is visible and countable.

**Visual:** requests stack up in a visible pile at the node's intake. A short pile is fine; a growing pile
is the hockey-stick beginning; an overflowing pile is dropped traffic spilling onto the floor. **You
should be able to see the queue before you see the alert.**

### The red tide / backpressure
See §7.1.

**Visual:** saturation crawls backward along the flow as a red wash, one hop at a time, with each hop
snapping taut. **FX_CascadeRipple.**

### Health as colour temperature
Not a health bar.

**Visual:** a healthy node is neutral-lit; a stressed node warms toward orange; a failing node goes red
and desaturates; a dead node goes grey and unlit. **Temperature reads instantly and never needs a
number.**

### Money as motion
Cash should always be moving somewhere.

**Visual:** **coin motes** arc from conversions to the balance. **Drain lines** run continuously from the
balance to the cost sinks (power, payroll, transit) as thin falling streams — **you can literally see
your burn rate.** The balance is a **liquid column**, not a number, so its level is felt. A **burn/earn
balance scale** tips visibly. **Payday** is a synchronized pulse and a distinct sound. An **invoice
slip** prints and slides into the AR tray.
**Interacts with:** §6.4 — the invoice calendar's rhythm should be audible and visible.

### Upgrades visible on the object
No upgrade is invisible.

**Visual:** added drives, an extra PSU, a new NIC, a bigger heatsink, a faceplate change, more lights.
**The object's history is readable from its face**, which makes upgrading feel like accumulating rather
than incrementing.

### The connection-made click
Small, crisp, repeated thousands of times.

**Visual/audio:** the cable snaps into the port with a detent animation and a satisfying click, and the
link lights on both ends negotiate (amber → green). **Get this one interaction right and the whole game
feels good.**

### Defense firing
Every defense has a distinct verb.

**Visual:** a rate limiter **meters** (a valve with a visible drip rate). A WAF **inspects** (a scanning
lattice — **FX_LatticeFlash**). A scrubbing centre **filters** (a wave that passes through and leaves
only cyan — **FX_ScrubWave**). A firewall **gates** (a physical barrier drops). A honeypot **lures** (a
false door that opens invitingly). A tarpit **slows** (attackers sink into thick amber — **FX_TarPit**).
**Never a generic projectile.** The defense should look like what it does.

### Recovery
The most satisfying animation set.

**Visual:** **FX_Reseat** (a device slides out, slides back, and POSTs). **FX_LightsOn** (an LED
sequence walking left to right as a unit comes up). **FX_FleetSync** (a configuration change rippling
across a fleet in a wave). **FX_GhostSolidify** (a restored dataset fading from a ghost into solid).
Cache warming shows the cache filling from the bottom.

### Degraded mode
The system visibly shedding.

**Visual:** disabled features grey out *in the world* — the recommendation service dims, the search node
goes dark, the site preview window switches to a simplified layout. **You see exactly what your customers
are no longer getting.**

### The sieve
Filtering made obvious.

**Visual:** mixed violet traffic pours into a defense; cyan passes through; magenta is caught and
accumulates visibly; amber (false positives) falls through the wrong side and is highlighted. **One image
that explains the entire defensive tradeoff.**

### Breaker trip / power events
**FX_BreakerTrip:** a snap, a chain of devices going dark in physical order along the circuit, and the
amp meter dropping to zero. **The most brutal two-second animation in the game.**

### Data loss and corruption
**FX_SealDrain:** a storage volume's fill level draining away with its seal breaking. **FX_FrostBloom:**
ransomware crystallizing across files. **FX_RansomOverlay:** the customer-facing view replaced by the
ransom note. **FX_ForensicDesat:** during investigation the world desaturates except the evidence trail.
**FX_DriftPile:** bit-rot rendered as a slow accumulation of drifted, discoloured blocks.

### Attack telegraphs and the radar sweep
See §8.5. **FX_PrismSplit:** a single incoming contact resolving into its constituent sources as
analysis completes.

### The empty lane
The quietest and most devastating visual in the game.

**Visual:** perfect infrastructure, all green, humming — and **no visitors at all.** Reputation zero
(§6.10) renders as silence and stillness. **No text needed.**

### Build satisfaction and the boot sequence
Placing a thing should feel like installing a thing.

**Visual:** the ghost solidifies, rails click, power connects, fans spin up with an audible ramp, POST
lights sequence, then idle. **Ten seconds of ceremony for a meaningful purchase, one second for a small
one.**

### Damage feedback on infrastructure
Towers take visible wear.

**Visual:** a stressed server's fan blur increases and its thermal tint rises. A drive that's failing
shows a flickering amber LED *before* it dies. A switch under attack shows its port lights saturating.
**Pre-failure warnings are visual, not textual**, and the observant player gets a free save.

### Money number feel
The counters must have physicality.

**Visual:** tabular figures so digits don't jitter; counters roll rather than snap; large gains get a
brief scale-up; losses get a red tick and a downward slide. **Never a silent instant change for anything
that matters.**

### Alert escalation visuals
Severity has a visual ladder.

**Visual:** info (a small dot in the ticker), warning (an amber pip on the object), minor (the object
outlines), major (a rack-level tint and a ticker entry), critical (a full alert card, the NOC wall goes
red, optional auto-pause). **Five clearly distinguishable steps**, never more.

### The quiet moment
Deliberate calm.

**Visual:** after an incident resolves, the game holds for a beat — alerts clear one by one with a soft
tick, colours settle back to neutral, the hum returns to idle pitch. **The exhale is as important as the
crisis**, and most games forget to build one.

---

## 8.8 UI and HUD

### The HUD skeleton
A fixed, minimal frame.

**How it works:** **Top bar** — money (liquid column), MRR ribbon, reputation face, date/clock, speed
controls. **Left** — build palette. **Right** — inspector for the current selection. **Bottom** — the
incident ticker and the alert stack. **Centre** — the world. Everything else is a drawer, an overlay, or
a hotkey. **Nothing on the HUD that the player cannot act on** (§6.8).

### The Site Preview Window
A small mock browser (or game client, or mail client, or phone) showing what a customer is experiencing
*right now.*

**How it works:** It renders slowly when you're slow, shows an error page when you're down, shows a
degraded layout in degraded mode, and shows the ransom note when you're ransomed. **The single best UI
idea in the document**: it converts every abstract metric into an emotional, concrete, customer's-eye
truth, and it **re-skins per hosting type** (a game client's connect screen, a mail client's inbox, a
transfer progress bar, an API response).

### The Pulse Strip
A single always-visible line showing the last N minutes of system health.

**Visual:** a compact sparkline-plus-colour band along the top or bottom. **Answers "is this normal?"
which is the actual first question of every incident.**

### The overlay wheel and its discipline
See §7.6. **One overlay at a time, exclusive palettes, a border tint naming the active lens.**

### The incident ticker
A running feed of what's happening, in plain language.

**Visual:** timestamped one-liners scrolling at the bottom. During an incident it's the narrative; in
peacetime it's atmosphere and foreshadowing. **Clickable — each line jumps the camera to its subject.**

### The Alert Stack
Alerts as a manageable pile, not a spam column.

**How it works:** Grouped by cause where possible, sorted by severity, each with an assign-a-hand button.
**Dependency-suppressed alerts collapse under their parent** with a count, which is the visual payoff
for buying alert tuning (§7.6).

### The Inspector Faceplate
Selecting anything shows a consistent panel.

**How it works:** A photo-real faceplate of the thing, its live stats, its connections, its config
sliders, its upgrade paths, and its recent history. **One layout for every object in the game** —
learnable once, used forever.

### The Timeline Ribbon
The level's history, scrubbed.

**Visual:** a horizontal ribbon of the whole level colour-coded by health, with incident markers,
deploy markers, and business events. Hovering shows what happened; it becomes the postmortem screen
(§6.9) at the end. **Built once, used in three places.**

### The graph drawer
Metrics on demand.

**How it works:** A pull-up drawer of the graphs you've unlocked, with p50/p95/p99 lines shown
separately (**p99 is the customer who is having a bad time and p50 hides them** — a lesson the UI
teaches by existing).

### The build catalogue / radial menu
Two options for placing things.

**How it works:** A categorized palette (organized by §5.5's branches) with search, or a radial menu at
the cursor for speed. Each entry shows cost, power draw, U height, **and the Surface icons it adds** —
the new attack types this build opens, shown as small threat glyphs on the build card. **P2 enforced at
the point of purchase.**

### Growing tooltips
Tooltips get richer as your knowledge does.

**How it works:** Early: "Some kind of bot." Later: full behaviour, weakness, expected damage, and your
history with it. **The UI itself is a progression system** (§5.4's Codex).

### The hover-card contract
Consistency rule.

**How it works:** Every hoverable thing shows a card with the same four regions: identity, current state,
relationships, and "what you can do." **Predictability beats density.**

### The minimap as whiteboard / NOC wall
Situational awareness at scale.

**Visual:** a schematic minimap that at high tiers becomes a **wall of screens in your NOC**, showing the
overview graphs, a world map, the ticker, and the "Days Since Last Outage" counter. **Diegetic UI that
is also functional UI.**

### The Ledger Drawer
The business panel.

**Visual:** a physical ledger/binder that pulls out, with tabs: P&L, Cash Flow, AR Aging, Per-Line
Margin, Cohorts. **Numbers in a game feel better when they live in an object.**

### The Cohort Wall
Retention made visual.

**Visual:** the classic cohort triangle, rendered as a grid of cells that fade as each month's customers
churn. **A steep fade is instantly, viscerally wrong** in a way a percentage never is.

### The Obligation Rail
What you owe, and when.

**Visual:** a horizontal rail of upcoming commitments — payroll, invoices due, SLA windows, audit
deadlines, contract renewals, the 90-day-lag consequences (§7.5). **Turns the future into a visible
object.**

### The SLA Meter
Live credit accrual.

**Visual:** a meter per contract showing uptime against commitment, turning red and **counting money
upward** during an incident. **Watching the credit number climb while you debug is the most motivating
UI element in the game.**

### The Concentration Donut
Revenue by customer.

**Visual:** a donut chart where one enormous slice is instantly legible as the risk it is. **The whale is
visible as a shape.**

### View filters: Money / Risk / Customer
Three lenses over the same board.

**How it works:** Tint the world by revenue contribution, by risk exposure, or by which customers are
affected. **The customer lens during an incident — "who is actually hurt right now" — is the one that
changes decisions.**

### The ticket queue panel
Support as a visible workload.

**Visual:** a stack of tickets with age colouring, sentiment icons, and the customer's value shown. **The
pile growing while you're in an incident is the truest thing in the game.**

### Time-of-day lighting and the clock
Diegetic time.

**Visual:** the office and the world light shifts through the day; 3AM is dark, blue, and lonely. **The
clock is a mood, not just a number.**

### Diegetic meters
Readouts as objects in the world.

**Visual:** amp meters on PDUs, temperature gauges on CRACs, a bandwidth graph on a wall-mounted screen,
a fill gauge on storage. **Information where the thing is** reduces the HUD's load.

### Notification discipline
Interruption is a budget.

**How it works:** Only a genuine sev-1 may take the screen. Everything else is a ticker line, a pip, or a
badge. **A game that constantly pops up modals is unplayable at Tier 5.**

### Tutorial-free onboarding
Teach with animated tooltips and events, not walls of text.

**How it works:** The first of anything shows a small animated demonstration on hover. Concepts arrive as
events that hurt slightly and then unlock their own counter (§5.1). **Almost no tutorial pop-ups.**

### Accessibility affordances
Non-negotiable.

**How it works:** Colour-blind-first iconography, a reduced-motion mode (animations become state changes),
audio redundancy for every critical visual alert, scalable UI, full keyboard navigation, tabular figures,
and high-contrast mode. **Also: pause exists and is always available.**

### Diagram export
Share your architecture.

**How it works:** Export the Wiring Mode view as a clean blueprint image with your company name in the
title block. **Free marketing and a genuine player-pride feature.**

---

## 8.9 Readability at scale

### Aggregate, don't shrink
The governing rule.

**How it works:** As counts grow, entities merge into a single glyph with a count and a worst-state
colour — never into tiny unreadable versions of themselves. **A rack of 40 servers is one object with a
health summary until you zoom to it.**

### The colour budget
See §8.2's Readability Budget. **Three alert colours on screen, maximum.**

### Alarm propagation and worst-state-wins
See §8.3. **You cannot hide a problem by zooming out.**

### Row rhythm and floor signage
Make a big room navigable.

**Visual:** consistent row spacing, aisle numbers painted on the floor, rack labels at the top of each
cabinet, hot/cold aisle colour hints in the floor paint. **Wayfinding design borrowed from real
datacenters, because it solves the same problem.**

### Tenant tinting
In multi-tenant and colo views, each customer gets a hue.

**Visual:** a subtle tint band on their gear. **Instantly answers "whose is this" and "who does this
outage affect."**

### The Heartbeat Sync
All ambient pulsing shares one clock.

**How it works:** Every idle pulse, LED blink and flow pulse is synchronized to one global heartbeat, so
the room breathes together. **Anything out of sync is instantly noticeable** — which turns
desynchronization itself into an alarm channel.

### The Quiet Frame Test
An art-direction acceptance test.

**How it works:** A screenshot of a healthy system must be **calm** — low contrast, slow motion, few
colours. If a healthy screenshot looks busy, the design has failed. **This single test protects the
entire visual language**, because it means anything loud is meaningful.

### Auto-LOD collapse and label culling
Text never overlaps.

**How it works:** Labels are ranked by importance and culled from the bottom as density rises; the
selected object's label always survives. **Never clip, never overlap, never shrink below legibility.**

### Contrast audit mode
A developer/player tool.

**How it works:** A mode that renders the screen as luminance only, to verify everything is
distinguishable without colour. **Ship it as an accessibility option too.**

### Zoom tiers change the metaphor
Each altitude is allowed its own language.

**How it works:** Z1 is a machine; Z3 is a diagram; Z4 is a map. **The transition animation is what
teaches that they're the same thing.**

---

## 8.10 Per-type and per-era visual identity

### The Business-Line Skin System
The mechanism for making 30 hosting types look distinct without 30 art budgets.

**How it works — the Five-Asset Skin Kit.** Each hosting line ships exactly five bespoke assets and
inherits everything else:
1. **A palette** (one dominant hue plus an accent that re-tints the whole substrate layer).
2. **A signature unit silhouette** (the one object that says "this is a GPU host / a tape vault / a game
   server").
3. **A visitor form** (what a customer looks like arriving — see §8.6).
4. **A signature meter** (the scarce-resource readout from §7.8, with its own dial art).
5. **An ambient FX + sound** (heat shimmer, tape robot motion, a busy signal, a fan wall).

**Five assets per line, one engine, total visual distinctiveness.** This is the single most practical
idea in the visual half of the document.

### The Business Line Placard
Each line's identity, stated.

**Visual:** a mounted placard per line with its name, icon, palette swatch and start date. Multi-line
companies get a wall of them; divested lines get theirs turned to face the wall (§5.7).

### Multi-line districts
Several businesses in one facility.

**Visual:** each line occupies a visually distinct district — different floor paint, lighting, density and
material language — separated by clear boundaries. **You should be able to tell which business you're
looking at from a single frame with the UI hidden.**

### Per-line visual identities (the catalogue)
*Condensed from the per-type specs; fuller descriptions live with each type in §1.3 and §4.10.*

- **Shared web hosting** — dense, uniform, beige-and-blue, hundreds of identical small units, a churning
  crowd of tiny visitors. Ambient: a wall of fans.
- **Managed WordPress** — same density, nicer materials, plugin icons visible as attached modules, a
  visible "updates pending" badge culture.
- **VPS / cloud** — translucent nested containers inside host chassis; you can see the tenants as
  glowing cells; noisy neighbours pulse and crowd their cell walls.
- **Dedicated / bare metal** — fewer, bigger, heavier units with real bezels; each has a customer name
  plate. Sparse and solid.
- **Colocation** — cages, mesh, padlocks, tenant tinting, **gear you can see but not touch** rendered
  slightly desaturated and behind a boundary. Ladder racking full of orange fibre.
- **Wholesale / hyperscale** — vast, empty, grey, under construction; cranes; a shell with capacity
  drawn in outline. **Scale as emptiness.**
- **Game servers** — dark room, saturated accent lighting, a **tick-rate oscilloscope** as the signature
  meter, player avatars as teardrops persisting in the node, a region ping map.
- **VoIP / SIP** — lines held between points as literal glowing strands; a **busy-signal lamp**; channel
  counters; a call-quality (MOS) needle.
- **Email** — envelope motes, reputation as a prominent gauge, blocklist status lamps, a deliverability
  funnel with a spam-folder side-channel.
- **DNS** — tiny, constant, everywhere; a **fine drizzle of query motes**; an anycast world map with
  glowing PoPs; p99 as the meter.
- **CDN** — a world map with cache nodes; content propagating outward in waves on publish; a **hit-ratio
  dial**; cold cache renders as dim nodes filling with light.
- **Object storage** — accretive; volumes that only ever grow; a fill gauge; erasure-coding shown as
  fragments distributed across nodes.
- **Backup / DR** — a **timeline as a second axis**, restore points as nodes in the past, a durability
  dial, and the green verified-restore stamp as the reward image.
- **Tape vaulting** — **the Tape Ballet**: a robotic library arm moving cartridges, barcode labels,
  courier vans, a physical vault door. Slow, mechanical, mesmerizing.
- **Video / streaming** — transcode ladders visualized as parallel bitrate lanes; a live viewer counter;
  egress rendered as a firehose.
- **Seedbox / file** — messy, fast, high-churn; takedown notices as paper; abuse tickets as a growing
  pile.
- **GPU / AI** — **heat shimmer and liquid cooling loops** as the signature; enormous power draw shown as
  thick gold cabling; a kW meter dominating the HUD; racks glowing orange in thermal view.
- **HPC / render** — a **job queue Gantt** as the main view; nodes lighting up in blocks as a job
  allocates; a deadline clock.
- **Crypto mining host** — shelves not racks, exposed boards, extreme density, brutal ambient noise, a
  power meter that never stops climbing, and tenant gear that is visibly nobody's problem but yours.
- **Kubernetes / PaaS** — abstracted; pods as small cells that reschedule and move between nodes
  automatically; **the board rearranges itself while you watch**, which is both accurate and unsettling.
- **Serverless** — almost nothing visible at rest; functions **bloom into existence** on invocation and
  vanish; cold starts render as a visible delay before the bloom.
- **DBaaS** — heavy, central, well-lit objects with visible replication streams and a lag counter.
- **Bulletproof** — dim, anonymous, no signage, offshore flags, prepaid crypto, an abuse-ticket queue
  that never empties, and a permanently amber legal-risk light.
- **Regulated (HIPAA/PCI/FedRAMP)** — badge readers, mantraps, camera domes, evidence seals, a
  **residency fence** drawn on the map, and an audit-readiness meter.
- **Dial-up ISP (era)** — modem banks with sequential lights, a busy-signal lamp, CRT UI chrome, beige
  everything, a POTS line count.
- **BBS / IRC / web ring (era)** — text-mode presentation, ANSI art, a member count, ring navigation
  arrows.
- **Satellite ground station** — a dish, a **sky window** showing pass schedules as arcs, weather as a
  first-class threat, and an antenna-time scarcity meter.
- **Edge / 5G MEC** — many tiny sites on a map rather than one big room; cabinets at the base of towers;
  a coverage overlay.
- **IoT backend** — enormous device counts as a fine dust of motes; ingest rate as the meter; a
  device-fleet health histogram.
- **Blockchain nodes** — a chain visualization, sync-height counters, storage that only grows.

### Era presentation shifts
Time changes everything above the substrate.

**How it works:** Each era re-skins the **UI chrome, palette, typography, and material language**: 1995
is beige plastic, CRT green, and Comic Sans warnings; 2005 is brushed aluminium, gradients, and glossy
web-2.0 chrome; 2015 is flat design, dark mode, and dashboards; 2025 is liquid cooling, glass, and
telemetry everywhere. **The gameplay is unchanged; the world has moved**, which is exactly the feeling
the era axis (§0.4) is for.

### The Frayed Line, The Busy Signal, and other signature FX
Each line gets one memorable motion.

**Visual:** the frayed cable (VoIP call quality degrading as a strand visibly unravels), the busy-signal
lamp (dial-up), the tape ballet, the heat shimmer, the pod reschedule, the serverless bloom, the
propagation wave. **One signature motion per line is what players will remember and describe to each
other.**

### Material and lighting language per line
Beyond palette.

**How it works:** Shared hosting is **plastic and fluorescent**. Colo is **steel, mesh, and cool
overheads**. GPU is **copper, glass, liquid, and orange glow**. Tape is **matte black, barcode white, and
a single work light**. Regulated is **clean white, camera domes, and even light**. Bulletproof is **dim,
uneven, and shadowed**. **Material carries mood faster than any HUD element.**

### Density signature
How full the room looks is part of the identity.

**How it works:** Shared hosting is crowded; dedicated is sparse; wholesale is nearly empty; crypto is
absurdly overpacked. **Density alone identifies a line from a thumbnail.**

### Visitor rhythm signature
The tempo of arrivals differs per line.

**How it works:** DNS is a constant fine drizzle. Game servers arrive in evening waves. Backup arrives in
a nightly burst. Colo arrives once a quarter, in a suit. **The rhythm of the screen is part of the
identity.**

### Cross-line collision control
Keeping multi-line screens readable.

**How it works:** When lines share a screen, palettes are constrained to their dominant hue only, and
shared UI stays neutral. **The Readability Budget applies across lines, not within each.**

### The Skin Preview Room
A gallery where you can see each line's look.

**How it works:** Unlocked lines appear as dioramas you can walk the camera through. Doubles as the
Company Museum (§9.4) and as the art team's showcase.

### The Signature Frame
Each line has one canonical camera angle.

**How it works:** Used for loading screens, the line placard, the level-select card and the score screen.
**A consistent hero image per line makes the game's variety legible in menus**, which is where players
actually perceive it.

### Weather and place at Z4
The world level has geography.

**Visual:** regional weather affecting satellite and microwave links, storm systems approaching a
facility, time zones showing which site is on night shift, and latency arcs between sites. **Makes
multi-site play feel like a map, not a spreadsheet.**

---

## 8.11 Audio

### The datacenter hum
The game's bed, and its most important instrument.

**How it works:** A continuous hum whose **pitch and volume track load.** Fans spin up as utilization
rises. The player learns to hear a problem before seeing it. **Ambient audio as telemetry is the most
underused idea in strategy games** and it is perfect here.

### The silence of power loss
The loudest sound in the game.

**How it works:** When utility power fails, **everything stops.** A moment of true silence, then the UPS
whine, then the generator turning over. **Silence as an alarm** is unforgettable.

### Fan spin-up and thermal audio
Heat is audible.

**How it works:** As a rack heats, its fans ramp — a rising whoosh that is unmistakable. Emergency
full-speed fans are genuinely alarming. **FX_FanSpindown** on shutdown is its melancholy counterpart.

### The drive click of death
Diagnostic audio.

**How it works:** A failing drive makes its characteristic click, audible if you're zoomed near it.
**A player who investigates the sound saves the array.**

### The breaker snap
A single sharp crack, followed by silence in one part of the room. **FX_BreakerTrip's** audio.

### Pager tones and severity
Interruption has a voice.

**How it works:** Distinct tones per severity; the sev-1 tone should raise your pulse. At 3AM the tone
plays over near-silence, which is the whole on-call experience in one design choice.

### The cash register rhythm
Money has a beat.

**How it works:** Conversions produce a tiny, pleasant tick; a healthy business is a steady rhythm of
them. **You can hear your conversion rate.** Payday is a distinct heavier sound; an SLA credit is a
sour note.

### Threat audio signatures
Each threat family has a sound.

**How it works:** A volumetric flood is a rising roar. A scanner is a rhythmic ping sweep. Ransomware is
a crystalline shatter. A mimic is **silent**, which is what makes it terrifying.

### Per-line sound palettes
Audio is part of the skin kit.

**How it works:** Tape libraries clunk and whirr. Dial-up has modem handshakes and busy signals. GPU
halls have liquid pumps. Game servers have a subtle UI-click culture. **The fifth asset in the
Five-Asset Skin Kit is a sound**, and it does more than the other four.

### Audio as visual redundancy
Accessibility requirement.

**How it works:** Every critical visual alert has an audio counterpart and vice versa, and the game is
fully playable with either channel off.

---

## 8.12 Presentation moments and the effects catalogue

### The Cold Open
Each level starts with a camera move.

**Visual:** the camera flies in from the world map, through the building, down the aisle, to your rack,
and the HUD assembles around it. **Ten seconds, sets the scale, never gets old.**

### The Wave Telegraph
The moment before the storm.

**Visual:** the horizon glows, the hum drops, the radar fills, the forecast widget escalates. **Dread is a
design goal.**

### The Save
The moment a defense works at the last possible second.

**Visual:** slow-motion for half a second, the scrubbing wave passing through, the flood dissolving, the
latency graph snapping back down, and a small "held" flourish. **Design the clutch moment deliberately or
it won't happen.**

### The 100% Uptime Stamp
The reward image.

**Visual:** a heavy rubber stamp slams onto the month's report. **Physical, loud, earned.**

### The Night Shot
An automatic beauty pass.

**Visual:** at 3AM, lights low, LEDs the only illumination, the hum at idle, a single desk lamp on.
**The game's wallpaper, generated from your own build.**

### Loading screens as rack diagrams
Useful and on-theme.

**Visual:** rack elevations, network diagrams and spec sheets with **loading-screen tips that are real
ops advice** (§9.3).

### The Credits Rack
The end.

**Visual:** credits presented as labels on a rack elevation, scrolling as the camera pans down.

### The FX catalogue
*A named, reusable effects vocabulary. Each is used in many places, which is how a small art budget
covers a large game.*

| Effect | What it shows |
|---|---|
| **FX_ScrubWave** | a filtering wave passing through mixed traffic, leaving only cyan |
| **FX_LatticeFlash** | inspection: a scanning lattice sweeping a request |
| **FX_SnapThread** | a connection breaking, the cable snapping and recoiling |
| **FX_FrostBloom** | encryption/ransomware crystallizing across data |
| **FX_DriftPile** | bit-rot: discoloured blocks accumulating over time |
| **FX_CoinArc** | revenue: a gold mote arcing to the balance |
| **FX_BouncePuff** | a visitor giving up and dissolving backward |
| **FX_BreakerTrip** | a circuit snapping, devices going dark in order |
| **FX_SealDrain** | data loss: a volume's fill draining, seal broken |
| **FX_CascadeRipple** | backpressure crawling upstream as a red tide |
| **FX_Reseat** | a device slid out and back, POSTing |
| **FX_FleetSync** | a config change rippling across a fleet |
| **FX_ForensicDesat** | investigation: the world desaturates except the evidence |
| **FX_GhostSolidify** | a restore completing: ghost to solid |
| **FX_TarPit** | attackers sinking into thick, slow amber |
| **FX_FanSpindown** | shutdown: fans winding down, lights fading |
| **FX_LightsOn** | power-up: LEDs sequencing, fans ramping, idle reached |
| **FX_RansomOverlay** | the customer view replaced by a ransom note |
| **FX_PrismSplit** | one contact resolving into its constituent sources |
| **FX_SagStrain** | a cable going taut and vibrating under saturation |

---

# 9. Anything else

## 9.1 Modes

### Campaign
The main arc: Tier 0 → Tier 6, across hosting types and eras.

**How it works:** A branching map where you choose which line of business to open next, so no two
campaigns cover the same types in the same order. **The variety engine is the campaign structure.**

### Endless / Survival ("The NOC")
One infrastructure, escalating forever.

**How it works:** Waves keep coming and keep getting harder; you build, adapt, and eventually fall.
Scored on how long and how well. **The mode people will actually play the most.**

### Incident Mode / Blitz Sev-1
A roguelite of single crises.

**How it works:** You're dropped into a pre-built (often badly built) system that is *already on fire*.
Diagnose and fix under a clock with limited hands. **Pure diagnosis, ten minutes, infinitely
replayable** — and the best possible showcase of what makes this game different.

### Daily Outage
One shared scenario per day, global leaderboard.

**How it works:** Everyone gets the same seed, the same broken system, the same clock. **Comparison
without multiplayer**, and a reason to open the game daily.

### Puzzle Mode ("Root Cause")
Hand-authored diagnosis puzzles.

**How it works:** Here are the symptoms, the graphs, and the logs. Name the cause. **A whole game inside
the game**, and the most educational mode.

### Sandbox / Architect / Lab / Zen
Unlimited money, no threats, build what you want.

**How it works:** For designing, for screenshots, for learning, and for the players who just want to
arrange a beautiful datacenter. **Chill Mode / Dream Datacenter** with optional ambient audio only.
**Never underestimate how many people will play only this.**

### Co-op NOC
Two to four players, one infrastructure, **asymmetric information.**

**How it works:** Each player sees different dashboards and must communicate. One is on the network
overlay, one on the application, one on the customer tickets. **Incident response is a communication
problem, and this mode is about that.** Plus **shift handoff**: a player leaving must brief the player
arriving, and what they forget to say is the next incident.

### Versus / Red vs Blue
One attacks, one defends, live.

**How it works:** The attacker picks tools and timing with a budget; the defender responds. Asymmetric,
tense, and it makes the bestiary matter.

### Async "Attack My Network"
Share your build as a puzzle for others to break.

**How it works:** Your topology is published; others attempt to find its weak point. You get their
attack reports. **Community-generated difficulty**, and a brutal architecture review.

### Competitive Market Mode
Multiple companies in one market.

**How it works:** Shared customer pool, price competition, poaching, reputation wars, and the occasional
suspiciously well-timed DDoS. **The business layer as a multiplayer game.**

### Attacker Mode / Reverse TD interlude
Play the other side.

**How it works:** A limited-length mode where you build a botnet and attack an NPC (or your past self's)
infrastructure. **Teaches defense by requiring offense**, and it's the best possible bestiary tutorial.

### Historical Scenarios
Recreations of famous infrastructure disasters, serial-numbers filed off.

**How it works:** The BGP leak that took out a continent. The certificate that expired. The
config-push that hit every region simultaneously. The cascading retry storm. **You know it's coming and
you still can't stop it**, which is the point.

### Campaign+ / New Game+
Replay with your knowledge and a fraction of your tech.

**How it works:** See §5.5's Rediscovery. Harder threats, tighter money, and the levels you found hard
are now the tutorial.

### Minimalist Mode
The whole game as a terminal.

**How it works:** CRT aesthetic (§8.1's style C), text-only, no diorama. **Costs almost nothing to build
on top of the existing simulation and will be somebody's favourite way to play.**

### Speedrun ("Zero to Nines" / "Zero to Rack")
Timed progression challenges.

**How it works:** Fastest to your own ASN. Fastest to 99.99% sustained. Fastest to a full rack.
**Leaderboards without needing a competitive mode.**

### Hardcore / Ironman
No pause, no undo, one save.

**How it works:** For the people who will ask for it. **Should be a toggle, not a separate mode.**

### The business modes
Different starting conditions that reweight the entire game.

**How it works:** **Bootstrapped** (no capital, pure cash discipline). **VC-Backed** (money now, growth
expectations, you can lose by growing slowly). **Roll-Up** (grow by acquisition; integration is the
game). **Turnaround** (inherit a failing company with angry customers and rotten infrastructure).
**Niche Run** (one hosting type only, mastery over breadth). **Solo Founder** (one pair of hands,
forever). **Ethics toggle** (whether the grey-area revenue is available at all).

### Line Draft
A roguelite structure for the variety engine.

**How it works:** At the start of a run you're offered three hosting lines and pick one; at each
milestone you draft another from three. **Every run is a different portfolio**, and the synergies and
antagonisms (§0.2) make the draft a real decision.

### One Building, Four Lines
A constrained challenge.

**How it works:** Run four different hosting types in one facility with shared power, cooling, staff and
network. **Every line's needs conflict** — the GPU line eats the power the colo tenants were promised.

### Through the Eras
A campaign spanning decades.

**How it works:** Start as a dial-up ISP in 1995 and survive to a GPU host in 2025, transitioning your
business four times. **The most ambitious mode here and the best premise.**

### Landlord vs Tenant
The same facility, two perspectives.

**How it works:** Play a level as the colo operator, then replay it as one of your own tenants, with all
the information asymmetry that implies. **The most illuminating design in the document about what
hosting actually is.**

### Blind Mode
No monitoring at all, by design.

**How it works:** You only learn about problems from customer tickets. **A short, horrifying, extremely
effective way to teach why observability exists.**

### The Same Outage Six Ways
A showcase mode.

**How it works:** One event — a power failure, say — played out as six different hosting types. **The
purest demonstration of the variety engine**, and a great demo/marketing mode.

### Photo Contest / Rack Gallery
Community screenshots.

**How it works:** Submit your build; browse others'. **Cheap, social, and it rewards the art.**

---

## 9.2 Twists and systemic wildcards

### Your Past Self Is The Boss
The best twist in the document.

**How it works:** A late-campaign level hands you the infrastructure *you* built in an earlier level, now
three years old, undocumented, and someone else's problem — which is to say, yours again. **Every
shortcut you took is now a monster.** Nothing else in the genre does this.

### The Ghost of the Previous Admin
Inherited systems come with a person's fingerprints.

**How it works:** In acquisition and turnaround levels, you find their labels, their scripts, their
naming convention, and a sticky note reading **"DO NOT REMOVE — ASK DAVE."** Dave left in 2019. **You
have to decide whether to touch it**, and the game never tells you what it does.

### The Legacy Box
The server with 1,847 days of uptime.

**How it works:** It runs something important. Nobody knows what. It has never been patched. **Rebooting
it may be the end of it**, and every month you don't, the risk grows. One of the load-bearing files on
it is a shell script named `fix.sh` that nobody wrote.

### Documentation as a mechanic
Writing things down is a purchasable action with compounding returns.

**How it works:** Documented systems have lower MTTR, survive staff turnover, and make delegation
possible. Undocumented systems are fine until the person leaves. **The most undervalued real practice,
correctly valued in a game.**

### Technical debt as visible substance
See §7.7.

**Visual:** it accumulates as physical mess — cable spaghetti, sticky notes, stacked boxes, dust, a
whiteboard nobody has erased. **You can see how much debt you're carrying by looking at the room.**

### The On-Call Clock
Someone is always carrying the pager.

**How it works:** A visible rotation. The person on call has reduced effectiveness the next day. Being
on call yourself is cheapest and burns *you*. **Sustainability as a mechanic.**

### The Vacation mechanic
Staff must take time off.

**How it works:** Refusing causes burnout; allowing it removes a hand for two weeks and reveals your bus
factor. **The most humane mechanic here and one of the sharpest** — you find out what only one person
knew when that person is on a beach.

### Rubber Duck
A diegetic hint system.

**How it works:** Explain the problem to the duck on your desk; the act of selecting the symptoms to
describe narrows the possibilities. **A hint system disguised as a real debugging technique**, which is
charming and also true.

### The 3AM toggle
Choose whether to be woken.

**How it works:** Page-me-for-everything (fast response, burnout) vs page-me-for-sev-1-only (rest,
slower response, the occasional very bad morning). **A setting that is a personality.**

### Vendor Personalities
Suppliers as recurring characters.

**How it works:** The reliable-but-expensive one. The cheap one whose gear fails at 18 months. The one
with great support and terrible availability. The one that gets acquired and becomes bad. **Relationships
you build and get burned by** (§5.4).

### Regulatory Weather
Rules change over time.

**How it works:** New data-protection law, new sanctions regime, new energy regulation, a new compliance
framework everyone suddenly demands. **The environment changes without asking you.**

### Community Bug Reports
Your customers find your problems.

**How it works:** Attentive customers report real issues before your monitoring does — if you've been good
enough to them that they bother. **Reputation as a monitoring system.**

### The Threat You Can Hire
Yesterday's attacker is today's security engineer.

**How it works:** After beating a specific attacker archetype, you may be able to hire them. Expensive,
morally ambiguous, extremely effective, and occasionally a disaster.

### Defend Someone Else
A guest level.

**How it works:** A friend's, a customer's, or a rival's infrastructure — built by someone with different
habits — and you have to work in it. **Reading someone else's architecture under pressure is the real
job.**

### The Dependency Web
You depend on other providers who have their own outages.

**How it works:** Your DNS provider, your CDN, your payment processor, your monitoring vendor, your cloud
region. **When theirs goes down, yours does, and you can do nothing but post a status update.** The most
helpless and most realistic twist available.

### Shared World Events
Global events affecting all players at once.

**How it works:** A major vulnerability disclosed, a submarine cable cut, a cloud region down, an energy
price spike. **Everyone is patching the same weekend**, which is exactly how it feels.

### The Seasonal Calendar
The year has a shape.

**How it works:** Black Friday, the holiday freeze, tax season, back-to-school, summer slump, the
January renewal wave. **Predictable if you look, brutal if you don't.**

### The Hidden Dependency
See §2.9 and §7.2.

### "It's Always DNS" (with a counter)
The joke, made mechanical.

**How it works:** A meaningful share of incidents genuinely trace to DNS, and the game keeps a visible
counter of how many times it was. **A running gag that is also a statistic.**

### Difficulty via Honesty ("Sysadmin Mode")
Turn off the helpful lies.

**How it works:** No alert aggregation, no root-cause hints, no auto-diagnosis, no dependency
suppression. **Raw logs and your own judgment.** The name is the whole pitch.

### The Pivot
Change what business you're in, mid-run.

**How it works:** See §5.6. Expensive, slow, and occasionally the only way to survive an era change.

### You Can Fire Customers
The most cathartic verb in the game.

**How it works:** Terminate an unprofitable, abusive or dangerous account. Costs revenue and a possible
public complaint; saves support hands, abuse risk and morale. **Every real operator knows exactly which
customer this is.**

### The marketing site on your own infra
Dogfooding, with consequences.

**How it works:** Your own website is hosted on your own infrastructure. When you go down, your status
page goes down too — **unless you had the sense to host it somewhere else**, which is a purchasable
lesson.

### The customer who becomes a competitor
They learn from you and leave.

**How it works:** A large technical customer gradually builds their own infrastructure, then competes
with you, then poaches your staff. **Nothing you can do about it, which is the lesson.**

### The acquisition offer you should refuse
Not every exit is good.

**How it works:** An offer arrives with terms that look great and an earnout that will never pay. **The
diligence report (§6.9) is available if you bother to read it.**

### The time-delayed consequence engine
The systemic version of the 90-day lag.

**How it works:** Many actions schedule their effects into the future — some good, some bad, some
forgotten. **The game's most distinctive feel: you are always living inside the consequences of a
decision you've stopped thinking about.**

### The Camera Is a Camera
Diegetic viewing.

**How it works:** The game view is your CCTV/monitoring system. Losing power or network to a site means
**losing the view of it** — you're flying blind on a part of your own company. **Terrifying and elegant.**

### The Investor Dashboard Lie
Two sets of numbers.

**How it works:** You control what the investor dashboard emphasizes. Presenting selectively is
available, effective, and carries a risk of being discovered at the next diligence. **The ethics dial
with a spreadsheet.**

### You Are the Attacker's Target Board
Perspective flip.

**How it works:** Occasionally the game shows your infrastructure as it appears in an attacker's
reconnaissance tooling — your exposed services, your stale software versions, your employee names from
LinkedIn. **Seeing yourself from outside is the single best security lesson available.**

### Weather Affects Everything
Physical world, physical consequences.

**How it works:** Heat waves stress cooling and raise power prices. Storms threaten utility power and
microwave links. Floods threaten basements (where the generators are). Cold snaps spike regional demand.
**A datacenter is a building, and buildings have weather.**

### The Hardware Lottery
Not all identical machines are identical.

**How it works:** Some units from a batch are simply worse — thermal outliers, early failures, firmware
oddities. **A small random factor that makes fleet management feel real** and rewards paying attention to
per-unit telemetry.

### Reputation Has a Face
Personify the abstract.

**How it works:** Reputation is shown as a small cast of recurring characters — a trade journalist, a
prominent customer, a forum regular — whose opinion of you visibly changes and who show up at the worst
times. **Far more affecting than a bar.**

### The Line That Eats You
A business line that grows past your ability to run it.

**How it works:** The line you opened opportunistically becomes 60% of your support load and 15% of your
revenue. **The divest decision is genuinely hard** because it's still growing.

### The Hardware Afterlife
See §6.6's cascade.

**How it works:** Follow a single server through its whole life: premium dedicated → budget dedicated →
VPS node → backup target → lab box → scrap, with a certificate of destruction. **A game that tracks one
machine's biography is a game people write about.**

### Reverse Colo
You become the tenant.

**How it works:** A level where *you* rent space in someone else's building and discover what it's like
to file a ticket for a reboot and wait. **Empathy as a mechanic**, and it makes your own colo levels
better.

### "Works On My Machine"
A card you can play once.

**How it works:** Closes a ticket without fixing anything. The problem comes back, bigger. **Pure joke,
pure mechanic.**

### The Missing Screw
Small physical friction.

**How it works:** Occasionally a task takes longer because the rail kit is wrong, the screw is missing,
the cable is six inches too short, or the box is on the wrong side of the room. **The tiny indignities
are what make it feel real.**

### The Beeping Server
A minigame everyone will recognize.

**How it works:** Something in the room is beeping. You have to find it by walking the camera through the
aisles, listening to the stereo pan. **Absurd, delightful, and completely authentic.**

### The Compliance Theater Meter
Real security vs the appearance of it.

**How it works:** Two separate stats. You can pass audits with theater and still be insecure; you can be
genuinely secure and fail an audit on documentation. **The gap between them is one of the sharpest
observations in the document.**

### The One Customer Who Is Always Right
A named recurring account.

**How it works:** They're big, they're demanding, they're often actually correct, and they will eat one
hand permanently. **Firing them is an option and you will think about it constantly.**

---

## 9.3 Humour and tone

### Recognition comedy, not parody
The governing rule.

**How it works:** The humour comes from accuracy — the ticket that says "it's slow" and nothing else, the
customer who changed something and swears they didn't, the label maker that ran out of tape halfway
through the row. **It should make an operator laugh and a civilian merely smile.** Never punch down at
customers; the joke is always the situation.

### The ticket text generator
Endless procedural support tickets.

**How it works:** Subject lines from real life: "URGENT!!! site down (it is not down)", "can you make it
faster", "I deleted something, can you get it back", "what is my password", "my developer left", "is
this a virus?" Per-line ticket packs give each hosting type its own comedy: **game hosting** gets
"someone griefed my base, can you roll back the world"; **email** gets "my mail goes to spam and it is
your fault"; **colo** gets "can your tech plug in this thing I FedEx'd you, instructions are on a
napkin"; **backup** gets "I need the file from Tuesday. No, the other Tuesday."

### Server naming and the label maker
The naming convention arc.

**How it works:** You choose a scheme early — Greek gods, Star Wars, colours, functional (`web01`),
cheeses. By Tier 4 you have hundreds and your early scheme has become a liability. **A running joke that
becomes an operational problem**, which is exactly the right kind of joke for this game.

### Vendor personalities
See §9.2. Their sales emails are a whole comedy channel.

### The Intern's Log
A diary that appears between levels.

**How it works:** An intern's increasingly world-weary observations about what you've been doing.
**Cheap, characterful, and the best place to put the game's voice.**

### Achievements
A list that is itself the joke.

**How it works:** *"It Was DNS." "Works On My Machine." "We Have Backups (Untested)." "Five Nines, Zero
Sleep." "Nobody Got Paged." "Blameless." "The Rebuild." "Bus Factor: Three." "Load Bank Tested." "Amps
Not Us." "Restored In Anger." "Zero Findings." "Nobody Missed A Pass." "Warmed The Pool." "Still
Running." "The Tour." "Toll Free." "Ask Dave." "Days Since Last Outage: 1."*

### The fictional trade press
A ticker of industry news.

**How it works:** Competitor announcements, acquisitions, outage coverage (sometimes yours), breathless
coverage of whatever the current hype cycle is. **World-building and mechanics at once** — the news
sometimes tells you what's coming.

### The status page euphemism ladder
Escalating honesty.

**How it works:** "Investigating elevated error rates" → "Degraded performance" → "Partial outage" →
"Major outage" → "We are aware of the fire." **The player chooses the wording and the customers notice
the gap between the words and reality.**

### The office set dressing
Where the warmth lives.

**How it works:** The cat that sits on the warm equipment (and occasionally causes an incident). The
coffee machine that shares a circuit with something important. The one beige server nobody will replace.
Cable colours nobody agreed on. The gong for closing a big deal. The **"Days Since Last Outage"**
whiteboard with a number you have to erase. The Comic Sans warning sign from 1998 that nobody has taken
down.

### Loading-screen tips that are real advice
Useful humour.

**How it works:** Actual operational wisdom, delivered dryly. *"A backup you haven't restored is a
hypothesis." "The failover you never tested is not a failover." "It is always DNS. Except when it's
MTU."*

### Staff chatter
Ambient dialogue.

**How it works:** Your team talks during quiet moments and during incidents. What they say during a
cascade is the game's best writing opportunity. **Nobody panics; everyone is tired.**

### The rm -rf moment
The game's most careful joke.

**How it works:** A confirmation dialog that you will one day click through too fast. **It must be
survivable** — that's what backups are for — and it must be nobody's fault but yours.

---

## 9.4 Meta systems

### The Playbook
Your accumulated runbooks, carried between runs.

**How it works:** Procedures you wrote become permanently available, slightly improved each time you use
them. **Meta-progression that is literally institutional knowledge.**

### The Alumni Network
Staff who left stay in the world.

**How it works:** They turn up at other companies, refer candidates to you, recommend you to customers,
or compete with you — depending on how you treated them. **The most elegant long-term consequence system
here.**

### The Annual Report
An end-of-year document.

**How it works:** A generated report with your financials, your uptime, your biggest incidents, your best
and worst customers, and a letter from the CEO (you). **Highly shareable, and it makes a year of play
feel like a chapter.**

### Industry Benchmarks
Compare yourself to the field.

**How it works:** "Your churn is 4.2%; the industry median is 2.8%." Sourced from aggregate player data
or from authored norms. **Context turns a number into a judgment.**

### The Company Museum
A permanent gallery of your history.

**How it works:** Every line you ever ran, every era you lived through, your first server, your worst
outage, your biggest customer, the Wall of Ghosts. **It's the Skin Preview Room (§8.10), the trophy rack
(§5.8), the scrapbook (§5.4) and the postmortem archive in one place** — and it's the single best reason
to keep a long save.

### The Company Wall
The small version: certificates, framed press clippings, the first dollar.

### Procedural label text
Detail that generates itself.

**How it works:** Asset tags, rack labels, port labels, cable tags, and warning stickers all generate
consistent, readable, in-fiction text. **At Z1 the world should reward reading.**

### The diegetic settings menu
Options as a physical object.

**How it works:** The settings are on a clipboard, or a BIOS screen, or a terminal. **Consistency of
fiction, top to bottom.**

### Screenshot watermark
Shareable by default.

**How it works:** Screenshots carry a small tasteful corner card with your company name, tier, uptime and
date. **Free marketing, and players like the artifact.**

### Modding the skin kit
The five-asset structure is a modding surface.

**How it works:** Because a hosting line is five assets plus a ruleset card, **players can author new
hosting types** — which is the perfect community content format for this game.

### The tutorial is a job
Onboarding as fiction.

**How it works:** You're hired at a small host; your first tasks are real tasks; your "training" is a
senior engineer telling you to go look at something. **No tutorial UI at all.**

### The Ending camera move
How a run finishes.

**How it works:** Whatever the ending — acquisition, institution, collapse — the camera pulls back
through the building, out of the city, to the world, and the lights of your sites stay on (or don't).
**One shot, earned.**

---

## 9.5 The educational angle

### Field Notes
An in-game encyclopedia written in plain language.

**How it works:** Every concept the game models — the 95th percentile, PUE, RAID levels, BGP, RPKI,
anycast, quorum, error budgets, deferred revenue, the queueing curve — gets a short, genuinely accurate
entry, unlocked when you encounter it. **A game that teaches this material by accident is worth more than
one that teaches it on purpose.**

### The "was that real?" tag
Authenticity, marked.

**How it works:** Entries and events carry a small tag saying whether the mechanic reflects real
practice, is a simplification, or is a liberty taken for fun. **Enormous trust-builder with the audience
who will care most.**
**Tension:** ⚔️ The game-design lens warns that too much accuracy-flagging breaks immersion. Mitigation:
it lives in the Codex, never in the world.

### Import Your Own Topology
The power-user feature.

**How it works:** Describe your real (or imagined) infrastructure and the game builds a level from it.
**Every ops team will want to do this to their own stack**, and the results will be shared widely.

### The community scenario editor
See §9.7.

### Educational mode
A classroom variant.

**How it works:** Slower, more explanatory, with the Field Notes surfaced inline and the Puzzle Mode as
the core. **A real market for this exists** and it costs little beyond what's already built.

---

## 9.6 Design guardrails

*Rules to hold the design to, collected from across the lenses.*

### Every tower has a downside
**No pure upgrades.** Everything you build adds capability *and* surface, cost, or complexity (P2). If a
build has no downside, it isn't finished.

### No optimal build order
The correct build must depend on the level, the hosting type, the threat mix and your money. **If the
community finds one build order, the design has failed.**

### Lose slowly
See §6.10's soft-over-hard rule. **You should always understand why you lost, and have seen it coming.**

### Teach through loss, never through text
See §5.1. **The game should have almost no tutorial pop-ups.**

### The three-clock rule
See §7.9. **Exactly three timers.**

### Make the boring thing beautiful
The unglamorous correct actions — documentation, drills, patching, dunning, cable management, blanking
panels — must have the **best animations, the best sounds, and the most satisfying feedback in the
game.** This is the single most important tonal decision available, because it's what makes the game
*about* something.

### Asset reuse discipline
The Five-Asset Skin Kit (§0.2, §8.10) and the FX catalogue (§8.12) exist so that variety is cheap.
**Any new content that requires more than five bespoke assets needs to justify itself.**

### Accessibility as a feature, not a setting
Colour-blind-first, motion-optional, audio-redundant, keyboard-complete, pause-always. **These also make
the game more readable for everyone** (§8.2's Diamond/Circle/Triangle law).

### Peacetime must be valuable
P3. If the player is bored between waves, the design has failed. **Drills, debt, documentation,
forecasting and tuning are the peacetime game.**

### The reward is letting things through
P9. Most tower defense rewards killing. **This game rewards service** — the score is how many visitors
you served, not how many threats you stopped.

### Nothing has an immediate result
P10. Builds take time, changes take effect after a delay, business decisions land in 90 days.
**Anticipation is the game.**

---

## 9.7 Long-tail and stretch ideas

### Level editor and workshop
Author scenarios, share them.

**How it works:** Define the topology, the threat waves, the customers, the money, the constraints, and
the hosting type. **Given the ruleset-card structure, a scenario is a small data file** — which makes
this unusually cheap for what it returns.

### Spectator and replay
Watch a run back.

**How it works:** Full replay with a scrubbable timeline (§8.8's Timeline Ribbon), overlay switching, and
commentary markers. **Perfect for teaching, for postmortems, and for streaming.**

### Seasonal live events
Shared world events on a calendar.

**How it works:** A month-long "Black Friday" event; a "Patch Tuesday" weekend; a global vulnerability
that everyone responds to at once. **Community cohesion without multiplayer.**

### Company culture as a stat
The soft thing, made mechanical.

**How it works:** Culture affects hiring, retention, incident behaviour, and whether people tell you bad
news early. **Blameless postmortems, sustainable on-call and documentation all feed it** — and a company
where people hide problems fails in a distinctive, awful way.

### Real uptime leaderboard
A long-horizon competition.

**How it works:** Longest continuous simulated uptime across a persistent save. **The streak is the
score**, and losing it hurts in a way no number does.

### The screensaver / idle view
The game as ambience.

**How it works:** A slow camera drifting through your datacenter, lights blinking, hum playing, numbers
ticking. **Half the audience for this game would leave it running on a second monitor**, and that is a
feature worth shipping.

### Mobile companion / status page
A second-screen conceit.

**How it works:** A phone-shaped view of your company's status page and alerts. Possibly literal, as an
actual companion app; possibly just a diegetic in-game device. **The on-call fantasy completed.**

### Franchise and multi-company play
The far horizon.

**How it works:** Run a group of companies, or license your platform to others, or become the upstream
for NPC hosts who then have their own outages that become yours. **Tier 7, if it ever exists.**
